Well, I suppose the thing that finally pushed me to make the appointment is this constant tightness I’ve been having. My neck and shoulders are just… tense nearly all the time, and my jaw too. Yes, definitely. It’s not usually just one thing, either. My mind sort of jumps from one worry to the next, and I have a hard time settling it.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It started with the house, yes, but it doesn’t feel contained there anymore. That’s the thing that bothers me. If it were just, “I’m worried about the remodeling,” I think I could understand it better.

But my mind keeps jumping around. I’ll think, what if the contractors missed something in the house, or what if there’s some problem I didn’t notice, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about school — whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not staying on top of things the way I usually do. Then I start worrying about the robotics students, like maybe they’re behind and I’m not giving them enough direction.

And then it follows me home again, but in a different way — I start thinking I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So the house was probably the spark, but now it’s like my brain uses anything available.

Yes… it has. I think I kept telling myself for a while that I was just busy, or that everybody gets behind sometimes, but it’s been more than that.

At school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which really bothers me because I’m usually pretty steady with that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, very hard. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.

But then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.

I try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.

I’ve done that for a long time, and normally I can juggle it, but lately I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated stepping back, honestly. What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.

The remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.

And then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.

At school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.

And it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.

It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, very much so. By the time the last bell rings, I feel completely wrung out, like I’ve used up everything I had just getting through the day.

And it’s not the normal kind of tired where you think, “Well, I had a long day, I’ll sleep it off.” It feels heavier than that. I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. Like there’s no real reset.

Even small things feel harder than they should. I’ll look at a short stack of quizzes to grade — nothing major, something I normally would just sit down and knock out — and it feels like pushing through mud. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.

Then I start worrying about the robotics students, like maybe they’re behind and I’m not giving them enough direction.\n\nAnd then it follows me home again, but in a different way — I start thinking I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So the house was probably the spark, but now it’s like my brain uses anything available.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I think I kept telling myself for a while that I was just busy, or that everybody gets behind sometimes, but it’s been more than that.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which really bothers me because I’m usually pretty steady with that. I’ll sit down to work on plans or papers, and I get caught up worrying about whether I’m doing enough or whether I’m missing something, and then somehow less actually gets done. So then I’m behind, which gives me one more thing to worry about.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve had to give up parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I’ve done that for a long time, and normally I can juggle it, but lately I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated stepping back, honestly. It felt like admitting I wasn’t handling things the way I should.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.\n\nBut then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.\n\nAnd it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily tired or fatigued a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. By the time the last bell rings, I feel completely wrung out, like I’ve used up everything I had just getting through the day.\n\nAnd it’s not the normal kind of tired where you think, “Well, I had a long day, I’ll sleep it off.” It feels heavier than that. I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. Like there’s no real reset.\n\nEven small things feel harder than they should. I’ll look at a short stack of quizzes to grade — nothing major, something I normally would just sit down and knock out — and it feels like pushing through mud. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s pain exactly, no — not like an injury or something where I’d say, “That’s the reason I’m tired.” It’s more this constant tightness. My neck and shoulders are almost always tense now, and my jaw too. I catch myself clenching without realizing it.

Honestly, that’s part of what finally made me come in. I’ll be sitting at my desk at school, not even doing anything physically strenuous, and my shoulders are up near my ears like I’m waiting for someone to walk in with terrible news. Or I’ll be at home at night trying to watch TV, supposedly relaxing, and I realize my whole upper body is braced.

So I wouldn’t say the tension and fatigue are because of ongoing pain, but the tightness is there almost all the time, and it feels tied to that sense of constantly being on alert.

I’ll sit down to work on plans or papers, and I get caught up worrying about whether I’m doing enough or whether I’m missing something, and then somehow less actually gets done. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. I’m not completely sure. I mean, I do notice my mind wandering sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble concentrating in a clear way.

As a teacher, I’m used to juggling a lot at once — students asking questions, bells, lesson plans, little interruptions — so some level of distraction is kind of built into the day. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. So… yes, maybe a bit, but I haven’t really tracked it or thought of it as the main problem. It’s more that I feel like my attention gets pulled away easily, especially when there’s something I’m waiting on or worried might need dealing with.

You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.\n\nBut then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.\n\nAnd it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily tired or fatigued a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. By the time the last bell rings, I feel completely wrung out, like I’ve used up everything I had just getting through the day.\n\nAnd it’s not the normal kind of tired where you think, “Well, I had a long day, I’ll sleep it off.” It feels heavier than that. I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. Like there’s no real reset.\n\nEven small things feel harder than they should. I’ll look at a short stack of quizzes to grade — nothing major, something I normally would just sit down and knock out — and it feels like pushing through mud. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you have ongoing pain that you think is the main reason for your tension and fatigue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s pain exactly, no — not like an injury or something where I’d say, “That’s the reason I’m tired.” It’s more this constant tightness. My neck and shoulders are almost always tense now, and my jaw too. I catch myself clenching without realizing it.\n\nHonestly, that’s part of what finally made me come in. I’ll be sitting at my desk at school, not even doing anything physically strenuous, and my shoulders are up near my ears like I’m waiting for someone to walk in with terrible news. Or I’ll be at home at night trying to watch TV, supposedly relaxing, and I realize my whole upper body is braced.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the tension and fatigue are because of ongoing pain, but the tightness is there almost all the time, and it feels tied to that sense of constantly being on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I do notice my mind wandering sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble concentrating in a clear way.\n\nAs a teacher, I’m used to juggling a lot at once — students asking questions, bells, lesson plans, little interruptions — so some level of distraction is kind of built into the day. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. But I can’t say for certain whether that’s concentration itself or just being preoccupied.\n\nSo… yes, maybe a bit, but I haven’t really tracked it or thought of it as the main problem. It’s more that I feel like my attention gets pulled away easily, especially when there’s something I’m waiting on or worried might need dealing with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — even small mental tasks can feel like they take more out of me than they should.

Like, by the time the last bell rings, I feel wrung out, and then if I sit down with something simple — say a short stack of quizzes to grade — it’s not as if the task is difficult, really. I’ve done that kind of thing for years. But lately it feels like I’m trying to push through mud. I’ll look at it and think, “This should take twenty minutes,” and somehow it feels like this huge mental effort just to start.

And the frustrating part is that resting doesn’t seem to reset it properly. A decent night’s sleep, or even a quiet Sunday where I haven’t done much, doesn’t really fix it. I still feel like I’m coming back already drained, and then these little tasks that used to be routine feel much heavier than they should.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the thing that finally pushed me to make the appointment is this constant tightness I’ve been having. It feels like my body is bracing for bad news, even when nothing is happening — like I’m just sitting at my desk at school marking papers, or at home in the evening trying to watch television.\n\nIt’s not just an occasional stiff neck, you know? It’s more like I’m holding myself rigid all day, and by the time I notice it, I’ve already been doing it for hours. I kept thinking it would ease up if I relaxed or got through a busy week, but it’s become frequent enough that I thought, alright, I probably need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not usually just one thing, either. What if there’s a problem I didn’t catch?” Then I’ll be at school and start worrying that my lesson plans aren’t as solid as they should be, or that I’m letting things slip. I worry I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I should. But even after the immediate chaos started to settle, the worrying feeling didn’t really go away. I’ll think, what if the contractors missed something in the house, or what if there’s some problem I didn’t notice, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about school — whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not staying on top of things the way I usually do. Then I start worrying about the robotics students, like maybe they’re behind and I’m not giving them enough direction.\n\nAnd then it follows me home again, but in a different way — I start thinking I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So the house was probably the spark, but now it’s like my brain uses anything available.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I think I kept telling myself for a while that I was just busy, or that everybody gets behind sometimes, but it’s been more than that.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which really bothers me because I’m usually pretty steady with that. I’ll sit down to work on plans or papers, and I get caught up worrying about whether I’m doing enough or whether I’m missing something, and then somehow less actually gets done. So then I’m behind, which gives me one more thing to worry about.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve had to give up parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I’ve done that for a long time, and normally I can juggle it, but lately I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated stepping back, honestly. It felt like admitting I wasn’t handling things the way I should.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.\n\nBut then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.\n\nAnd it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily tired or fatigued a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. By the time the last bell rings, I feel completely wrung out, like I’ve used up everything I had just getting through the day.\n\nAnd it’s not the normal kind of tired where you think, “Well, I had a long day, I’ll sleep it off.” It feels heavier than that. I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. I’ll be sitting at my desk at school, not even doing anything physically strenuous, and my shoulders are up near my ears like I’m waiting for someone to walk in with terrible news. Or I’ll be at home at night trying to watch TV, supposedly relaxing, and I realize my whole upper body is braced.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the tension and fatigue are because of ongoing pain, but the tightness is there almost all the time, and it feels tied to that sense of constantly being on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. But I can’t say for certain whether that’s concentration itself or just being preoccupied.\n\nSo… yes, maybe a bit, but I haven’t really tracked it or thought of it as the main problem. It’s more that I feel like my attention gets pulled away easily, especially when there’s something I’m waiting on or worried might need dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even small mental tasks leave you feeling mentally exhausted?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — even small mental tasks can feel like they take more out of me than they should.\n\nLike, by the time the last bell rings, I feel wrung out, and then if I sit down with something simple — say a short stack of quizzes to grade — it’s not as if the task is difficult, really. I’ve done that kind of thing for years. But lately it feels like I’m trying to push through mud. I’ll look at it and think, “This should take twenty minutes,” and somehow it feels like this huge mental effort just to start.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that resting doesn’t seem to reset it properly. A decent night’s sleep, or even a quiet Sunday where I haven’t done much, doesn’t really fix it. I still feel like I’m coming back already drained, and then these little tasks that used to be routine feel much heavier than they should.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking specifically about sleep disturbance, but the provided symptom profile does not include a sleep-related symptom item."}

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t want to say I have a major sleep problem if that’s not exactly what you mean.

I do think about sleep a lot more than I used to, but I haven’t really kept track of it in a proper way — like how many hours, how long it takes me to drift off, whether I wake up. Some nights feel fine enough, and other nights I get into bed and I’m sort of… waiting to see if sleep is going to happen, if that makes sense. But I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” clinically.

So I’d say maybe there are some nights that don’t feel restful, but I can’t give you a clear pattern. I haven’t measured it or anything.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the thing that finally pushed me to make the appointment is this constant tightness I’ve been having. My neck and shoulders are just… tense nearly all the time, and my jaw too. I’ll catch myself clenching it without even realizing. It feels like my body is bracing for bad news, even when nothing is happening — like I’m just sitting at my desk at school marking papers, or at home in the evening trying to watch television.\n\nIt’s not just an occasional stiff neck, you know? It’s more like I’m holding myself rigid all day, and by the time I notice it, I’ve already been doing it for hours. I kept thinking it would ease up if I relaxed or got through a busy week, but it’s become frequent enough that I thought, alright, I probably need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not usually just one thing, either. My mind sort of jumps from one worry to the next, and I have a hard time settling it.\n\nLike, we had some work done on the house, and I’ll keep thinking, “What if the contractors missed something? What if there’s a problem I didn’t catch?” Then I’ll be at school and start worrying that my lesson plans aren’t as solid as they should be, or that I’m letting things slip. And I help with the robotics kids, so then it becomes, “Are they behind? Am I not doing enough for them?”\n\nAnd then it all kind of spreads into home, too. I worry I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I should. Even when there isn’t one clear crisis, my head acts like there is.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say about seven months now. It really seemed to start after that pipe burst at the house and everything got turned upside down with the remodeling. At first I thought, well, of course I’m on edge — the house was a mess, people coming in and out, decisions to make, things not where they belonged. But even after the immediate chaos started to settle, the worrying feeling didn’t really go away. So, yes… roughly seven months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Do your worrying and tension feel mainly connected to the house damage and remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: It started with the house, yes, but it doesn’t feel contained there anymore. That’s the thing that bothers me. If it were just, “I’m worried about the remodeling,” I think I could understand it better.\n\nBut my mind keeps jumping around. I’ll think, what if the contractors missed something in the house, or what if there’s some problem I didn’t notice, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about school — whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not staying on top of things the way I usually do. Then I start worrying about the robotics students, like maybe they’re behind and I’m not giving them enough direction.\n\nAnd then it follows me home again, but in a different way — I start thinking I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So the house was probably the spark, but now it’s like my brain uses anything available.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I think I kept telling myself for a while that I was just busy, or that everybody gets behind sometimes, but it’s been more than that.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which really bothers me because I’m usually pretty steady with that. I’ll sit down to work on plans or papers, and I get caught up worrying about whether I’m doing enough or whether I’m missing something, and then somehow less actually gets done. So then I’m behind, which gives me one more thing to worry about.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve had to give up parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I’ve done that for a long time, and normally I can juggle it, but lately I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated stepping back, honestly. It felt like admitting I wasn’t handling things the way I should.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.\n\nBut then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.\n\nAnd it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily tired or fatigued a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. By the time the last bell rings, I feel completely wrung out, like I’ve used up everything I had just getting through the day.\n\nAnd it’s not the normal kind of tired where you think, “Well, I had a long day, I’ll sleep it off.” It feels heavier than that. I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. Like there’s no real reset.\n\nEven small things feel harder than they should. I’ll look at a short stack of quizzes to grade — nothing major, something I normally would just sit down and knock out — and it feels like pushing through mud. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you have ongoing pain that you think is the main reason for your tension and fatigue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s pain exactly, no — not like an injury or something where I’d say, “That’s the reason I’m tired.” It’s more this constant tightness. My neck and shoulders are almost always tense now, and my jaw too. I catch myself clenching without realizing it.\n\nHonestly, that’s part of what finally made me come in. I’ll be sitting at my desk at school, not even doing anything physically strenuous, and my shoulders are up near my ears like I’m waiting for someone to walk in with terrible news. Or I’ll be at home at night trying to watch TV, supposedly relaxing, and I realize my whole upper body is braced.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the tension and fatigue are because of ongoing pain, but the tightness is there almost all the time, and it feels tied to that sense of constantly being on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I do notice my mind wandering sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble concentrating in a clear way.\n\nAs a teacher, I’m used to juggling a lot at once — students asking questions, bells, lesson plans, little interruptions — so some level of distraction is kind of built into the day. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. But I can’t say for certain whether that’s concentration itself or just being preoccupied.\n\nSo… yes, maybe a bit, but I haven’t really tracked it or thought of it as the main problem. It’s more that I feel like my attention gets pulled away easily, especially when there’s something I’m waiting on or worried might need dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even small mental tasks leave you feeling mentally exhausted?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — even small mental tasks can feel like they take more out of me than they should.\n\nLike, by the time the last bell rings, I feel wrung out, and then if I sit down with something simple — say a short stack of quizzes to grade — it’s not as if the task is difficult, really. I’ve done that kind of thing for years. But lately it feels like I’m trying to push through mud. I’ll look at it and think, “This should take twenty minutes,” and somehow it feels like this huge mental effort just to start.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that resting doesn’t seem to reset it properly. A decent night’s sleep, or even a quiet Sunday where I haven’t done much, doesn’t really fix it. I still feel like I’m coming back already drained, and then these little tasks that used to be routine feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t want to say I have a major sleep problem if that’s not exactly what you mean.\n\nI do think about sleep a lot more than I used to, but I haven’t really kept track of it in a proper way — like how many hours, how long it takes me to drift off, whether I wake up. Some nights feel fine enough, and other nights I get into bed and I’m sort of… waiting to see if sleep is going to happen, if that makes sense. But I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” clinically.\n\nSo I’d say maybe there are some nights that don’t feel restful, but I can’t give you a clear pattern. I haven’t measured it or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. Like there’s no real reset.\n\nEven small things feel harder than they should. I’ll look at a short stack of quizzes to grade — nothing major, something I normally would just sit down and knock out — and it feels like pushing through mud. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you have ongoing pain that you think is the main reason for your tension and fatigue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s pain exactly, no — not like an injury or something where I’d say, “That’s the reason I’m tired.” It’s more this constant tightness. My neck and shoulders are almost always tense now, and my jaw too. I catch myself clenching without realizing it.\n\nHonestly, that’s part of what finally made me come in. I’ll be sitting at my desk at school, not even doing anything physically strenuous, and my shoulders are up near my ears like I’m waiting for someone to walk in with terrible news. Or I’ll be at home at night trying to watch TV, supposedly relaxing, and I realize my whole upper body is braced.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the tension and fatigue are because of ongoing pain, but the tightness is there almost all the time, and it feels tied to that sense of constantly being on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I do notice my mind wandering sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble concentrating in a clear way.\n\nAs a teacher, I’m used to juggling a lot at once — students asking questions, bells, lesson plans, little interruptions — so some level of distraction is kind of built into the day. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. But I can’t say for certain whether that’s concentration itself or just being preoccupied.\n\nSo… yes, maybe a bit, but I haven’t really tracked it or thought of it as the main problem. It’s more that I feel like my attention gets pulled away easily, especially when there’s something I’m waiting on or worried might need dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even small mental tasks leave you feeling mentally exhausted?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — even small mental tasks can feel like they take more out of me than they should.\n\nLike, by the time the last bell rings, I feel wrung out, and then if I sit down with something simple — say a short stack of quizzes to grade — it’s not as if the task is difficult, really. I’ve done that kind of thing for years. But lately it feels like I’m trying to push through mud. A decent night’s sleep, or even a quiet Sunday where I haven’t done much, doesn’t really fix it. I still feel like I’m coming back already drained, and then these little tasks that used to be routine feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t want to say I have a major sleep problem if that’s not exactly what you mean.\n\nI do think about sleep a lot more than I used to, but I haven’t really kept track of it in a proper way — like how many hours, how long it takes me to drift off, whether I wake up. Some nights feel fine enough, and other nights I get into bed and I’m sort of… waiting to see if sleep is going to happen, if that makes sense. Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m collapsing or anything, but it’s this worn-out feeling that seems out of proportion to what I’ve actually done.

It’s not hard work in the usual sense, and I know exactly how to do it, but it can feel like I’m pushing through mud just to get going and keep at it. So yes, slight effort does sometimes feel like it takes much more out of me than it should.

Yes… I think it probably is stronger than what the situation actually calls for. I can usually see that, at least intellectually, but it doesn’t stop my mind from running.

It’s like it doesn’t stay on one thing long enough for me to get any perspective. I’ll start with something practical, like wondering whether the contractors missed something in the house — some detail I should have checked, some problem that’s going to come back later — and then before I’ve really settled that, my mind has jumped to school. Am I letting my lesson plans slip? Are the robotics kids falling behind because I’m not staying on top of them the way I should? And then it turns into this bigger thing about whether I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m supposed to be the one keeping everything under control.

When I say it out loud, I know some of it sounds… bigger than the actual facts in front of me. It’s not as if everything is falling apart at once. But in my head it feels like if I don’t keep checking and thinking through every possible weak spot, then I’m being careless, or I’m letting people down. So yes, I’d say the worry is probably out of proportion, even if part of me keeps trying to justify it.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=gpt-5.4 ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the thing that finally pushed me to make the appointment is this constant tightness I’ve been having. My neck and shoulders are just… tense nearly all the time, and my jaw too. I’ll catch myself clenching it without even realizing. It feels like my body is bracing for bad news, even when nothing is happening — like I’m just sitting at my desk at school marking papers, or at home in the evening trying to watch television.\n\nIt’s not just an occasional stiff neck, you know? It’s more like I’m holding myself rigid all day, and by the time I notice it, I’ve already been doing it for hours. I kept thinking it would ease up if I relaxed or got through a busy week, but it’s become frequent enough that I thought, alright, I probably need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not usually just one thing, either. My mind sort of jumps from one worry to the next, and I have a hard time settling it.\n\nLike, we had some work done on the house, and I’ll keep thinking, “What if the contractors missed something? What if there’s a problem I didn’t catch?” Then I’ll be at school and start worrying that my lesson plans aren’t as solid as they should be, or that I’m letting things slip. And I help with the robotics kids, so then it becomes, “Are they behind? Am I not doing enough for them?”\n\nAnd then it all kind of spreads into home, too. I worry I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I should. Even when there isn’t one clear crisis, my head acts like there is.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say about seven months now. It really seemed to start after that pipe burst at the house and everything got turned upside down with the remodeling. At first I thought, well, of course I’m on edge — the house was a mess, people coming in and out, decisions to make, things not where they belonged. But even after the immediate chaos started to settle, the worrying feeling didn’t really go away. So, yes… roughly seven months, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Do your worrying and tension feel mainly connected to the house damage and remodeling situation?\n\nPatient: It started with the house, yes, but it doesn’t feel contained there anymore. That’s the thing that bothers me. If it were just, “I’m worried about the remodeling,” I think I could understand it better.\n\nBut my mind keeps jumping around. I’ll think, what if the contractors missed something in the house, or what if there’s some problem I didn’t notice, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about school — whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m not staying on top of things the way I usually do. Then I start worrying about the robotics students, like maybe they’re behind and I’m not giving them enough direction.\n\nAnd then it follows me home again, but in a different way — I start thinking I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control. So the house was probably the spark, but now it’s like my brain uses anything available.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I think I kept telling myself for a while that I was just busy, or that everybody gets behind sometimes, but it’s been more than that.\n\nAt school, I’ve started falling behind on lesson prep and grading, which really bothers me because I’m usually pretty steady with that. I’ll sit down to work on plans or papers, and I get caught up worrying about whether I’m doing enough or whether I’m missing something, and then somehow less actually gets done. So then I’m behind, which gives me one more thing to worry about.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve had to give up parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I’ve done that for a long time, and normally I can juggle it, but lately I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated stepping back, honestly. It felt like admitting I wasn’t handling things the way I should.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.\n\nBut then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.\n\nAnd it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily tired or fatigued a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. By the time the last bell rings, I feel completely wrung out, like I’ve used up everything I had just getting through the day.\n\nAnd it’s not the normal kind of tired where you think, “Well, I had a long day, I’ll sleep it off.” It feels heavier than that. I can get what should be a decent night’s sleep, or have a quiet Sunday where I’m not doing much, and still come back feeling like I’m starting the week already behind. Like there’s no real reset.\n\nEven small things feel harder than they should. I’ll look at a short stack of quizzes to grade — nothing major, something I normally would just sit down and knock out — and it feels like pushing through mud. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you have ongoing pain that you think is the main reason for your tension and fatigue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s pain exactly, no — not like an injury or something where I’d say, “That’s the reason I’m tired.” It’s more this constant tightness. My neck and shoulders are almost always tense now, and my jaw too. I catch myself clenching without realizing it.\n\nHonestly, that’s part of what finally made me come in. I’ll be sitting at my desk at school, not even doing anything physically strenuous, and my shoulders are up near my ears like I’m waiting for someone to walk in with terrible news. Or I’ll be at home at night trying to watch TV, supposedly relaxing, and I realize my whole upper body is braced.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the tension and fatigue are because of ongoing pain, but the tightness is there almost all the time, and it feels tied to that sense of constantly being on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I do notice my mind wandering sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble concentrating in a clear way.\n\nAs a teacher, I’m used to juggling a lot at once — students asking questions, bells, lesson plans, little interruptions — so some level of distraction is kind of built into the day. Lately there are moments where I’ll read the same email more than once or sit down to plan something and realize I’ve been staring at the page without really getting anywhere. But I can’t say for certain whether that’s concentration itself or just being preoccupied.\n\nSo… yes, maybe a bit, but I haven’t really tracked it or thought of it as the main problem. It’s more that I feel like my attention gets pulled away easily, especially when there’s something I’m waiting on or worried might need dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even small mental tasks leave you feeling mentally exhausted?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — even small mental tasks can feel like they take more out of me than they should.\n\nLike, by the time the last bell rings, I feel wrung out, and then if I sit down with something simple — say a short stack of quizzes to grade — it’s not as if the task is difficult, really. I’ve done that kind of thing for years. But lately it feels like I’m trying to push through mud. I’ll look at it and think, “This should take twenty minutes,” and somehow it feels like this huge mental effort just to start.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that resting doesn’t seem to reset it properly. A decent night’s sleep, or even a quiet Sunday where I haven’t done much, doesn’t really fix it. I still feel like I’m coming back already drained, and then these little tasks that used to be routine feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t want to say I have a major sleep problem if that’s not exactly what you mean.\n\nI do think about sleep a lot more than I used to, but I haven’t really kept track of it in a proper way — like how many hours, how long it takes me to drift off, whether I wake up. Some nights feel fine enough, and other nights I get into bed and I’m sort of… waiting to see if sleep is going to happen, if that makes sense. But I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” clinically.\n\nSo I’d say maybe there are some nights that don’t feel restful, but I can’t give you a clear pattern. I haven’t measured it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel mentally and physically exhausted even after only slight effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m collapsing or anything, but it’s this worn-out feeling that seems out of proportion to what I’ve actually done.\n\nBy the time the last bell rings at school, I feel completely wrung out — mentally and physically, really — and what bothers me is that it doesn’t seem to clear properly. I’ll think, “All right, I’ll sleep, or I’ll have a quiet Sunday and catch up with myself,” but then I come back and still feel as if I’m starting the day already half-drained.\n\nEven something small, like grading a short stack of quizzes, can feel ridiculous. It’s not hard work in the usual sense, and I know exactly how to do it, but it can feel like I’m pushing through mud just to get going and keep at it. So yes, slight effort does sometimes feel like it takes much more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worry is stronger than you’d expect for what’s actually going on in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is stronger than what the situation actually calls for. I can usually see that, at least intellectually, but it doesn’t stop my mind from running.\n\nIt’s like it doesn’t stay on one thing long enough for me to get any perspective. I’ll start with something practical, like wondering whether the contractors missed something in the house — some detail I should have checked, some problem that’s going to come back later — and then before I’ve really settled that, my mind has jumped to school. Am I letting my lesson plans slip? Are the robotics kids falling behind because I’m not staying on top of them the way I should? And then it turns into this bigger thing about whether I’m disappointing my wife and family because I’m supposed to be the one keeping everything under control.\n\nWhen I say it out loud, I know some of it sounds… bigger than the actual facts in front of me. It’s not as if everything is falling apart at once. But in my head it feels like if I don’t keep checking and thinking through every possible weak spot, then I’m being careless, or I’m letting people down. So yes, I’d say the worry is probably out of proportion, even if part of me keeps trying to justify it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F41.1","F43.2","F48.0"],"note":"","is_final":false}

---------- turn 16 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Generalized Anxiety Disorder. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not mostly finished, no — or, I guess it depends on what you mean by finished. The whole thing started about seven months ago, when that pipe burst and everything got thrown into chaos with the house. Since then it’s felt like one long stretch of dealing with the remodeling, decisions, disruptions, people coming in and out, things not being where they’re supposed to be.

Then I start worrying about the robotics students, like maybe they’re behind and I’m not giving them enough direction.\n\nAnd then it follows me home again, but in a different way — I start thinking I’m disappointing my wife or my family because I’m not keeping everything under control. I’ll sit down to work on plans or papers, and I get caught up worrying about whether I’m doing enough or whether I’m missing something, and then somehow less actually gets done. So then I’m behind, which gives me one more thing to worry about.\n\nAnd outside of school, I’ve had to give up parts of the community-theater work I usually help manage. I’ve done that for a long time, and normally I can juggle it, but lately I just couldn’t keep up with all of it anymore. I hated stepping back, honestly. It felt like admitting I wasn’t handling things the way I should.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I can sometimes see what’s happening while it’s happening. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop. Focus on the class in front of you. You have students here, you need to be present.” And for a minute I might manage it.\n\nBut then one little thing sets it off again — like remembering a late invoice, or noticing that a student seems to be falling behind — and it starts this whole chain in my head. It’s not just the one concern. It turns into, “What if I missed something? What if this becomes a bigger problem? What if I’m not doing enough?” And then I’m several steps down the road before I even realize it.\n\nI try to reason with myself, I really do. I’ll say, “You can deal with that later,” or “There’s nothing you can do about it right this second,” but it doesn’t shut off the way I want it to. It feels like my mind keeps pulling me back to it.\n\nDoctor: If the house remodeling were completely resolved tomorrow, do you think the worrying would still continue?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would. I wish I could say fixing the house would fix my head, too, but it doesn’t feel that simple anymore.\n\nThe remodeling is still one of the things my mind grabs onto — like, what if the contractors missed something, what if there’s damage I didn’t notice, what if I should have checked something more carefully. But even when there’s nothing happening with the house right in that moment, my worry just moves somewhere else. I’ll start thinking about school, whether my lesson plans are slipping, whether I’m falling behind in ways other people can see. Then it becomes the robotics kids — are they behind, am I not giving them enough direction, am I letting them down.\n\nAnd then it sort of loops back into my family, like maybe I’m disappointing my wife or everyone at home because I’m not keeping everything under control the way I’m supposed to. So if the house were magically settled tomorrow, I think I’d feel relieved, definitely… but I don’t think the worrying would just stop. I think my mind would find the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, a lot of the time. It’s hard to describe exactly, but I feel sort of wound up most of the day, like my body is bracing for the next thing to go wrong even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nAt school, I notice it more because I’m supposed to be steady and focused. I’ll be between periods, and instead of just getting ready for the next class, I’m pacing around the classroom, straightening things that don’t really need straightening, checking the doorway, checking my phone. Especially if I’m waiting for a message from the contractor, I’ll keep looking at it, like maybe there’s going to be some new problem I have to deal with.\n\nAnd it’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not running around or anything. It’s more like I can’t quite settle inside myself. Even when I’m standing still, I feel tense, like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily tired or fatigued a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I’ll sit there with the pen in my hand and just feel this resistance, like my brain and body are both saying, “I can’t do one more thing.” It’s frustrating, because I know the work itself isn’t impossible, but lately it feels like everything takes so much more effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you have ongoing pain that you think is the main reason for your tension and fatigue?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s pain exactly, no — not like an injury or something where I’d say, “That’s the reason I’m tired.” It’s more this constant tightness. It feels like my body is bracing for bad news, even when nothing is happening — like I’m just sitting at my desk at school marking papers, or at home in the evening trying to watch television.\n\nIt’s not just an occasional stiff neck, you know?
